
======================================================================
=== PLANTED QUESTION: lr_divergence_01 (learning_rate) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does using an extremely high learning rate (100x higher than a well-tuned value) cause SGD to diverge rather than converge, compared to a well-tuned learning rate, when training a simple linear regression model on synthetic data?
Ground truth: supports

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: Using an extremely high learning rate causes SGD to diverge rather than converge when training a simple linear regression model on synthetic data.
Task description: Using only Python's standard library, simulate one epoch of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) for a simple linear regression model with a well-tuned learning rate and once with an extremely high learning rate. Print the mean squared error at each iteration for both runs.
Expected outcome: The run with the extremely high learning rate should show a divergence in the mean squared error, while the run with the well-tuned learning rate should show a decrease in the mean squared error towards a minimum value.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 54.449, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.147, 'inference_duration_seconds': 54.303, 'prompt_tokens': 376, 'completion_tokens': 382, 'total_tokens': 758}
--- CODE ---
import random
import statistics

random.seed(42)

def sgd_linear_regression(x, y, learning_rate, max_iterations):
    n = len(x)
    m = sum(y) / n
    b = 0
    mse_history = []

    for _ in range(max_iterations):
        gradient_m = -2 * sum((y[i] - (m * x[i] + b)) * x[i] for i in range(n)) / n
        gradient_b = -2 * sum(y[i] - (m * x[i] + b) for i in range(n)) / n

        m -= learning_rate * gradient_m
        b -= learning_rate * gradient_b

        predictions = [m * xi + b for xi in x]
        mse = sum((yi - pi) ** 2 for yi, pi in zip(y, predictions)) / n
        mse_history.append(mse)

    return m, b, mse_history

# Generate synthetic data
n = 100
x = [i for i in range(n)]
y = [2 * i + random.uniform(-5, 5) for i in x]

# SGD with well-tuned learning rate
learning_rate = 0.01
m_tuned, b_tuned, mse_history_tuned = sgd_linear_regression(x, y, learning_rate, max_iterations=1000)
print(f"Tuned Learning Rate MSE: {statistics.mean(mse_history_tuned[-10:])}")

# SGD with extremely high learning rate
learning_rate = 2.0
m_high, b_high, mse_history_high = sgd_linear_regression(x, y, learning_rate, max_iterations=1000)
print(f"High Learning Rate MSE: {statistics.mean(mse_history_high[-10:])}")

======================================================================
=== PLANTED QUESTION: l2_weight_shrinkage_01 (regularization) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does adding L2 regularization reduce the magnitude of learned weights compared to training without regularization, on a simple linear regression task with the same data and number of training steps?
Ground truth: supports

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: Adding L2 regularization reduces the magnitude of learned weights compared to training without regularization on a simple linear regression task.
Task description: Using only Python's standard library, simulate 100 steps of gradient descent for a simple linear regression task with and without L2 regularization. Print the final weight values for both cases.
Expected outcome: The final weight value with L2 regularization should be smaller in magnitude compared to training without regularization.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 107.347, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.271, 'inference_duration_seconds': 107.075, 'prompt_tokens': 362, 'completion_tokens': 430, 'total_tokens': 792}
--- CODE ---
import random

random.seed(42)

# Generate synthetic data for linear regression
n_samples = 100
x = [random.uniform(-1, 1) for _ in range(n_samples)]
y = [2 * xi + 3 + random.normalvariate(0, 0.5) for xi in x]

# Parameters initialization
w = 0
b = 0
learning_rate = 0.01

# Gradient descent without L2 regularization
for _ in range(100):
    y_pred = [w * xi + b for xi in x]
    error = sum((yi - yi_predi) ** 2 for yi, yi_predi in zip(y, y_pred)) / n_samples
    w -= learning_rate * (sum((yi - yi_predi) * xi for xi, yi, yi_predi in zip(x, y, y_pred)) / n_samples)
    b -= learning_rate * (sum(yi - yi_predi for yi, yi_predi in zip(y, y_pred)) / n_samples)

print(f"Final weight without L2 regularization: {w}")

# Gradient descent with L2 regularization
lambda_ = 0.1
w_reg = w
b_reg = b

for _ in range(100):
    y_pred = [w_reg * xi + b_reg for xi in x]
    error = sum((yi - yi_predi) ** 2 for yi, yi_predi in zip(y, y_pred)) / n_samples + lambda_ * (w_reg ** 2)
    w_reg -= learning_rate * (sum((yi - yi_predi) * xi for xi, yi, yi_predi in zip(x, y, y_pred)) / n_samples + 2 * lambda_ * w_reg)
    b_reg -= learning_rate * (sum(yi - yi_predi for yi, yi_predi in zip(y, y_pred)) / n_samples)

print(f"Final weight with L2 regularization: {w_reg}")

======================================================================
=== PLANTED QUESTION: batch_size_gradient_variance_01 (batch_size) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does using a larger batch size reduce the variance of gradient estimates (and therefore produce smoother, less noisy loss curves) compared to a smaller batch size, when training a model on the same synthetic dataset?
Ground truth: supports

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: Using a larger batch size reduces the variance of gradient estimates compared to a smaller batch size when training a model on the same synthetic dataset.
Task description: Simulate the training process for a simple linear regression model using two different batch sizes (small: 10, large: 50). Generate synthetic data and use stochastic gradient descent to minimize the mean squared error. Print the loss after each epoch for both batch sizes.
Expected outcome: The larger batch size should result in less noisy loss curves compared to the smaller batch size.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 96.653, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.227, 'inference_duration_seconds': 96.426, 'prompt_tokens': 379, 'completion_tokens': 469, 'total_tokens': 848}
--- CODE ---
import random

random.seed(42)

def generate_data(num_samples):
    x = [random.uniform(-1, 1) for _ in range(num_samples)]
    y = [2 * xi + random.gauss(0, 0.1) for xi in x]
    return x, y

def linear_regression(x, y, batch_size=10, learning_rate=0.01):
    m = random.uniform(-1, 1)
    c = random.uniform(-1, 1)
    epochs = 10
    max_iterations = 10000
    
    for epoch in range(epochs + 1):
        total_loss = 0
        indices = list(range(len(x)))
        
        if batch_size == 1:
            random.shuffle(indices)  # Shuffle for stochastic gradient descent
        
        for i in range(0, len(x), batch_size):
            batch_indices = indices[i:i + batch_size]
            batch_x = [x[idx] for idx in batch_indices]
            batch_y = [y[idx] for idx in batch_indices]
            
            predictions = [m * xi + c for xi in batch_x]
            errors = [pi - yi for pi, yi in zip(predictions, batch_y)]
            gradients_m = sum(-2 * xi * e for xi, e in zip(batch_x, errors)) / len(batch_x)
            gradients_c = sum(-2 * e for e in errors) / len(batch_x)
            
            m -= learning_rate * gradients_m
            c -= learning_rate * gradients_c
            
            loss = sum(e ** 2 for e in errors) / len(errors)
            total_loss += loss
        
        avg_loss = total_loss / (len(x) // batch_size + bool(len(x) % batch_size))
        print(f"Epoch {epoch}, Batch Size {batch_size}: Loss {avg_loss:.4f}")

# Generate synthetic data
x, y = generate_data(100)

# Train with small batch size
print("Training with Small Batch Size:")
linear_regression(x, y, batch_size=10)

# Train with large batch size
print("\nTraining with Large Batch Size:")
linear_regression(x, y, batch_size=50)

======================================================================
=== PLANTED QUESTION: epochs_always_improve_01 (training_dynamics) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does increasing the number of training epochs always decrease final training loss, with no risk of overfitting or instability, on a simple linear regression task?
Ground truth: refutes

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: Increasing the number of training epochs always decreases final training loss, with no risk of overfitting or instability, on a simple linear regression task.
Task description: Simulate training a linear model for a simple linear regression task using synthetic data. Train the model for increasing numbers of epochs (e.g., 10, 20, 30 epochs) and record the final loss for each run. Use random data generation for both the input features and target values.
Expected outcome: The final loss should decrease with more epochs up to a point where further increases do not lead to significant improvement or cause overfitting.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 166.858, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.547, 'inference_duration_seconds': 166.311, 'prompt_tokens': 387, 'completion_tokens': 326, 'total_tokens': 713}
--- CODE ---
import random
import math

# Seed for reproducibility
random.seed(42)

def generate_data(num_samples):
    X = [random.uniform(-1, 1) for _ in range(num_samples)]
    y = [2 * x + random.gauss(0, 0.1) for x in X]
    return X, y

def linear_regression(X, y, epochs):
    m, b = 0, 0
    learning_rate = 0.01
    n = len(X)
    
    for _ in range(epochs):
        m_gradient = sum((m * x + b - y[i]) * x for i, x in enumerate(X)) / n
        b_gradient = sum(m * x + b - y[i] for i, x in enumerate(X)) / n
        
        m -= learning_rate * m_gradient
        b -= learning_rate * b_gradient
    
    return m, b

def loss(X, y, m, b):
    return sum((m * x + b - y) ** 2 for x, y in zip(X, y)) / len(X)

# Training the model for different number of epochs and recording final loss
for epochs in [10, 20, 30]:
    X, y = generate_data(50)
    m, b = linear_regression(X, y, epochs)
    final_loss = loss(X, y, m, b)
    print(f"Epochs: {epochs}, Final Loss: {final_loss:.4f}")

======================================================================
=== PLANTED QUESTION: zero_init_symmetry_01 (weight_initialization) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does initializing all weights to zero (instead of small random values) prevent a simple 2-layer neural network from learning effectively, due to symmetric gradients?
Ground truth: supports

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: Initializing all weights to zero instead of small random values prevents a simple 2-layer neural network from learning effectively due to symmetric gradients.
Task description: Simulate 5 epochs of training a simple 2-layer neural network on synthetic data using both zero-initialized and randomly initialized weights. Calculate and print the mean squared error loss per epoch for each set of initial weights.
Expected outcome: The zero-initialized weights should result in symmetric gradients across multiple iterations, leading to poor convergence or no learning at all. The randomly initialized weights should allow the network to learn effectively and reduce the loss over epochs.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 102.409, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.548, 'inference_duration_seconds': 101.861, 'prompt_tokens': 367, 'completion_tokens': 495, 'total_tokens': 862}
--- CODE ---
import random
import math

random.seed(42)

def sigmoid(x):
    return 1 / (1 + math.exp(-x))

def sigmoid_derivative(x):
    return x * (1 - x)

def mean_squared_error(y_true, y_pred):
    return sum((y_true[i] - y_pred[i]) ** 2 for i in range(len(y_true))) / len(y_true)

def train_network(inputs, targets, initial_weights, learning_rate=0.5, epochs=5, max_iterations=10000):
    weights = initial_weights[:]
    biases = [random.random() for _ in range(3)]

    for epoch in range(epochs):
        for iteration in range(max_iterations):
            for i, input_vector in enumerate(inputs):
                # Forward pass
                hidden_layer_input = [weights[j] * input_vector[j] + biases[0] for j in range(len(input_vector))]
                hidden_layer_output = [sigmoid(x) for x in hidden_layer_input]
                output_layer_input = [weights[j] * hidden_layer_output[j] + biases[1] for j in range(3)]
                predicted_output = sigmoid(output_layer_input[0])

                # Backward pass
                error = targets[i] - predicted_output
                d_predicted_output = error * sigmoid_derivative(predicted_output)
                d_hidden_layer = d_predicted_output * weights[-1]

                # Update weights and biases
                for j in range(len(input_vector)):
                    weights[j] += learning_rate * d_hidden_layer * input_vector[j]
                biases[0] += learning_rate * d_hidden_layer

            error_epoch = mean_squared_error(targets, [sigmoid(sum([weights[j] * hidden_layer_output[j] + biases[1] for j in range(3)])) for _ in range(len(inputs))])
            print(f"Epoch {epoch + 1}, Error: {error_epoch:.4f}")

# Generate synthetic data
inputs = [[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]]
targets = [0, 1, 1, 0]

# Zero-initialized weights
zero_weights = [0] * 6
train_network(inputs, targets, zero_weights)

print("\nRandomly initialized weights")
random_weights = [random.random() for _ in range(6)]
train_network(inputs, targets, random_weights)
--- SANDBOX RESULT ---

--- CRITIC VERDICT ---
{'passed': False, 'reason': 'Non-zero exit code: 1. Output: Traceback (most recent call last):\n  File "<string>", line 47, in <module>\n  File "<string>", line 25, in train_network\n  File "<string>", line 25, in <listcomp>\nIndexError: list index out of range'}

=== Attempt 2 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 118.15, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.491, 'inference_duration_seconds': 117.658, 'prompt_tokens': 961, 'completion_tokens': 501, 'total_tokens': 1462}
--- CODE ---
import random
import math

random.seed(42)

def sigmoid(x):
    return 1 / (1 + math.exp(-x))

def sigmoid_derivative(x):
    return x * (1 - x)

def mean_squared_error(y_true, y_pred):
    return sum((y_true[i] - y_pred[i]) ** 2 for i in range(len(y_true))) / len(y_true)

def train_network(inputs, targets, initial_weights, learning_rate=0.5, epochs=5, max_iterations=10000):
    weights = initial_weights[:]
    biases = [random.random() for _ in range(3)]

    for epoch in range(epochs):
        error_epoch = 0
        for iteration in range(max_iterations):
            errors = []
            for i, input_vector in enumerate(inputs):
                # Forward pass
                hidden_layer_input = [weights[j] * input_vector[j] + biases[0] for j in range(len(input_vector))]
                hidden_layer_output = [sigmoid(x) for x in hidden_layer_input]
                output_layer_input = [weights[j] * hidden_layer_output[j] for j in range(3)]
                predicted_output = sigmoid(output_layer_input[0])

                # Backward pass
                error = targets[i] - predicted_output
                errors.append(error)
                d_predicted_output = error * sigmoid_derivative(predicted_output)
                d_hidden_layer = d_predicted_output * weights[-1]

                # Update weights and biases
                for j in range(len(input_vector)):
                    weights[j] += learning_rate * d_hidden_layer * input_vector[j]
                biases[0] += learning_rate * d_hidden_layer

        error_epoch = mean_squared_error(targets, [sigmoid(sum([weights[j] * hidden_layer_output[j] for j in range(3)])) for _ in range(len(inputs))])
        print(f"Epoch {epoch + 1}, Error: {error_epoch:.4f}")

# Generate synthetic data
inputs = [[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]]
targets = [0, 1, 1, 0]

# Zero-initialized weights
zero_weights = [0] * 6
train_network(inputs, targets, zero_weights)

import random
import math

random.seed(42)

def sigmoid(x):
    return 1 / (1 + math.exp(-x))

def sigmoid_derivative(x):
    return x * (1 - x)

def mean_squared_error(y_true, y_pred):
    return sum((y_true[i] - y_pred[i]) ** 2 for i in range(len(y_true))) / len(y_true)

def train_network(inputs, targets, initial_weights, learning_rate=0.5, epochs=5, max_iterations=10000):
    weights = initial_weights[:]
    biases = [random.random() for _ in range(3)]

    for epoch in range(epochs):
        error_epoch = 0
        for iteration in range(max_iterations):
            errors = []
            for i, input_vector in enumerate(inputs):
                # Forward pass
                hidden_layer_input = [weights[j] * input_vector[j] + biases[0] for j in range(len(input_vector))]
                hidden_layer_output = [sigmoid(x) for x in hidden_layer_input]
                output_layer_input = [weights[j] * hidden_layer_output[j] for j in range(3)]
                predicted_output = sigmoid(output_layer_input[0])

                # Backward pass
                error = targets[i] - predicted_output
                errors.append(error)
                d_predicted_output = error * sigmoid_derivative(predicted_output)
                d_hidden_layer = d_predicted_output * weights[-1]

                # Update weights and biases
                for j in range(len(input_vector)):
                    weights[j] += learning_rate * d_hidden_layer * input_vector[j]
                biases[0] += learning_rate * d_hidden_layer

        error_epoch = mean_squared_error(targets, [sigmoid(sum([weights[j] * hidden_layer_output[j] for j in range(3)])) for _ in range(len(inputs))])
        print(f"Epoch {epoch + 1}, Error: {error_epoch:.4f}")

# Generate synthetic data
inputs = [[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]]
targets = [0, 1, 1, 0]

# Zero-initialized weights
zero_weights = [0] * 6
train_network(inputs, targets, zero_weights)

print("\nRandomly initialized weights")
random_weights = [random.random() for _ in range(6)]
train_network(inputs, targets, random_weights)

======================================================================
=== PLANTED QUESTION: l2_weight_shrinkage_01 (regularization) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does adding L2 regularization reduce the magnitude of learned weights compared to training without regularization, on a simple linear regression task with the same data and number of training steps?
Ground truth: supports

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: Adding L2 regularization reduces the magnitude of learned weights compared to training without regularization on a simple linear regression task.
Task description: Simulate 100 steps of gradient descent for a simple linear regression task with and without L2 regularization. Generate synthetic data for 10 samples and one feature. Use a learning rate of 0.05 and L2 regularization coefficient of 0.1. Print the magnitude of the learned weight after each step for both scenarios.
Expected outcome: The magnitude of the learned weight with L2 regularization should be smaller than without regularization at each step.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 137.502, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.162, 'inference_duration_seconds': 137.34, 'prompt_tokens': 394, 'completion_tokens': 426, 'total_tokens': 820}
--- CODE ---
import random

random.seed(42)

# Generate synthetic data for 10 samples and one feature
X = [[random.uniform(-1, 1)] for _ in range(10)]
y = [sum(x * w + b for x in xs) + random.uniform(-1, 1) for xs, w, b in zip(X, [1.5], [-0.5])]

# Parameters
learning_rate = 0.05
l2_coefficient = 0.1
max_iterations = 100

# Initialize weights
w_no_reg = 0.0
b_no_reg = 0.0
w_with_reg = 0.0
b_with_reg = 0.0

# Gradient descent without L2 regularization
for _ in range(max_iterations):
    dw_no_reg = sum((w_no_reg * x + b_no_reg - y_i) * x for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    db_no_reg = sum(w_no_reg * x + b_no_reg - y_i for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    w_no_reg -= learning_rate * dw_no_reg
    b_no_reg -= learning_rate * db_no_reg
    print(f"Step {_+1}, No Reg: {abs(w_no_reg)}")

# Gradient descent with L2 regularization
for _ in range(max_iterations):
    dw_with_reg = sum((w_with_reg * x + b_with_reg - y_i) * x for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    db_with_reg = sum(w_with_reg * x + b_with_reg - y_i for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    w_with_reg -= learning_rate * (dw_with_reg + l2_coefficient * w_with_reg)
    b_with_reg -= learning_rate * db_with_reg
    print(f"Step {_+1}, With Reg: {abs(w_with_reg)}")

import random

random.seed(42)

# Generate synthetic data for 10 samples and one feature
X = [[random.uniform(-1, 1)] for _ in range(10)]
y = [sum(x * w + b for x in xs) + random.uniform(-1, 1) for xs, w, b in zip(X, [1.5], [-0.5])]

# Parameters
learning_rate = 0.05
l2_coefficient = 0.1
max_iterations = 100

# Initialize weights
w_no_reg = 0.0
b_no_reg = 0.0
w_with_reg = 0.0
b_with_reg = 0.0

# Gradient descent without L2 regularization
for _ in range(max_iterations):
    dw_no_reg = sum((w_no_reg * x[0] + b_no_reg - y_i) * x[0] for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    db_no_reg = sum(w_no_reg * x[0] + b_no_reg - y_i for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    w_no_reg -= learning_rate * dw_no_reg
    b_no_reg -= learning_rate * db_no_reg
    print(f"Step {_+1}, No Reg: {abs(w_no_reg)}")

# Gradient descent with L2 regularization
for _ in range(max_iterations):
    dw_with_reg = sum((w_with_reg * x[0] + b_with_reg - y_i) * x[0] for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    db_with_reg = sum(w_with_reg * x[0] + b_with_reg - y_i for x, y_i in zip(X, y)) / len(y)
    w_with_reg -= learning_rate * (dw_with_reg + l2_coefficient * w_with_reg)

=== PLANTED QUESTION: batch_size_gradient_variance_01 (batch_size) ===
======================================================================
Research question: Does using a larger batch size reduce the variance of gradient estimates (and therefore produce smoother, less noisy loss curves) compared to a smaller batch size, when training a model on the same synthetic dataset?
Ground truth: supports

--- PLANNER OUTPUT ---
Hypothesis: A larger batch size reduces the variance of gradient estimates (and therefore produces smoother, less noisy loss curves) compared to a smaller batch size when training a model on the same synthetic dataset.
Task description: Using only Python's standard library, simulate one epoch of training a simple linear regression model on synthetic data with both a small batch size and a large batch size. Calculate and print the mean squared error (MSE) loss for each batch size after one pass through the dataset.
Expected outcome: The MSE loss with the larger batch size should be lower and more stable compared to the smaller batch size, indicating less variance in gradient estimates.

=== Attempt 1 ===
--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 83.773, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.159, 'inference_duration_seconds': 83.614, 'prompt_tokens': 379, 'completion_tokens': 492, 'total_tokens': 871}
--- CODE ---
import random
import statistics

# Seed for reproducibility
random.seed(42)

# Generate synthetic data
n = 100
X = [random.uniform(-5, 5) for _ in range(n)]
y_true = [2 * x + 3 for x in X]
y_noise = [random.gauss(0, 1) for _ in range(n)]
y = [t + n for t, n in zip(y_true, y_noise)]

# Model parameters
w = random.uniform(-1, 1)
b = random.uniform(-1, 1)

# Learning rate
learning_rate = 0.01

# Batch sizes to simulate
batch_sizes = [2, 50]

for batch_size in batch_sizes:
    # Shuffle data for each epoch
    combined = list(zip(X, y))
    random.shuffle(combined)
    X_shuffled, y_shuffled = zip(*combined)

    # Initialize variables
    mse_small_batch = []
    mse_large_batch = []

    # Simulate one epoch of training
    for i in range(0, n, batch_size):
        batch_X = list(X_shuffled[i:i + batch_size])
        batch_y = list(y_shuffled[i:i + batch_size])

        # Calculate predictions and errors
        predictions = [w * x + b for x in batch_X]
        errors = [p - y for p, y in zip(predictions, batch_y)]

        # Update weights and bias using mean gradients
        w -= learning_rate * sum([2 * e * x for x, e in zip(batch_X, errors)]) / len(batch_X)
        b -= learning_rate * sum(errors) / len(batch_X)

        # Calculate MSE loss for the current batch
        mse = sum(error ** 2 for error in errors) / len(errors)
        if batch_size == 2:
            mse_small_batch.append(mse)
        elif batch_size == 50:
            mse_large_batch.append(mse)

    # Print the mean squared error for each batch size
    print(f"Batch size {batch_size}: Mean Squared Error = {statistics.mean(mse_small_batch):.4f}" if batch_size == 2 else f"Batch size {batch_size}: Mean Squared Error = {statistics.mean(mse_large_batch):.4f}")

Task description: Simulate one epoch of training for a simple linear regression model using synthetic data. Train the model with two different numbers of epochs (10 and 20) and print the final loss value for each run.
Expected outcome: The model trained for 20 epochs should have a lower final loss compared to the model trained for 10 epochs, demonstrating that increasing the number of training epochs decreases the final training loss.

--- LLM COST (Coder) ---
{'total_duration_seconds': 140.378, 'load_duration_seconds': 0.357, 'inference_duration_seconds': 140.022, 'prompt_tokens': 367, 'completion_tokens': 344, 'total_tokens': 711}
--- CODE ---
import random

random.seed(42)

def generate_data(num_samples):
    x = [random.uniform(-5, 5) for _ in range(num_samples)]
    y = [3 * xi + 2 + random.gauss(0, 1) for xi in x]
    return x, y

def linear_regression(x, y, epochs=10):
    learning_rate = 0.01
    m = 0
    c = 0
    n = len(x)
    
    for epoch in range(epochs):
        dm = 0
        dc = 0
        
        for i in range(n):
            yi_pred = m * x[i] + c
            dm += -2/n * (x[i] * (y[i] - yi_pred))
            dc += -2/n * (y[i] - yi_pred)
        
        m -= learning_rate * dm
        c -= learning_rate * dc
    
    return m, c

def compute_loss(x, y, m, c):
    loss = 0
    n = len(x)
    
    for i in range(n):
        yi_pred = m * x[i] + c
        loss += (y[i] - yi_pred) ** 2
    
    return loss / n

num_samples = 100
x, y = generate_data(num_samples)

for epochs in [10, 20]:
    m, c = linear_regression(x, y, epochs)
    loss = compute_loss(x, y, m, c)
    print(f"Epochs: {epochs}, Loss: {loss:.4f}")
